By region: in the Delta the effect is the post (Long Huu, Cai Lay); in the coastal lowlands it is the mine and what it did to the Marines' relations with the hamlets (Dien Ban); in the Highlands it is small, the RF companies' Montagnards taught in a language that told them whose army it was, and the CIDG camps untouched; and in the Saigon belt it is the self-defence hamlets of 1968 and the deserters of Cu Chi.

## §4.N Net assessment and end state

The rung stays at (a), collapse, and this memorandum gives it no reason to move. The ladder's next rung needs a Republic whose state survives the withdrawal of American combat forces for longer than history's two years, and the basic course does not reach any of the mechanisms that decided that survival. It does not give the Republic an air arm it owns, it does not survive the 1974 appropriation any better than the force did, and it does not choose the corps commanders. A better private in an army with the historical generals and the historical aid vote is still in that army.

**The date of the fall, tested.** I estimate the fall at 30 April 1975 within a window of about 27 April to the first days of May, as before. The mechanisms in the cumulative force that pull it later are the ones meta-checkpoint 01 named, the better battalions at Duc Duc, Xuan Loc, Phan Rang and Cu Chi, the PAVN's greener divisions, the stronger held belt and, from memorandum 008, the domestic ammunition that stretched the last aid dollar; this memorandum adds trained privates who live through their wounds and hold their first ten minutes, which act in exactly the fights the PAVN bypassed in 1975. The mechanisms that pull it earlier are a President more certain his army could not hold alone and an army more dependent on fire it no longer had; this memorandum adds nothing to either, except that the families the soldiers went to in March were no better fed. The palace's orders and Hanoi's calendar dominate, as before.

**The critical path.** Tested against the palace's orders of 13–14 March, the 1st Corps' march against the rains and Minh's surrender, the basic course reaches none: the orders were taken in the palace, the march was the PAVN's, and the surrender was the President's. The collapse of I and II Corps came from families on the roads and contradictory orders, and a trained private goes to his family as surely as an untrained one. The delay it could buy is hours at Xuan Loc or Phan Rang, not the three weeks that would have put the assault into the rains.

**Running sums.** American lives saved by all provisions: about 3,400–6,900, the meta-checkpoint's 2,500–5,000 plus this memorandum's 900–1,900, a judgment resting on rates (two to four percent of killed in action for this memorandum's tourniquet), shown now at Albany and in the first wave of Tet; it is six to twelve percent of the historical dead and does not cross a band, because tolerance was spent by casualties of a scale and by Tet. RVNAF lives saved by this memorandum: about 4,000–12,000, two to five percent of the RVNAF's historical dead, below a band, shown at Thuan Loi and in the Delta towns at Tet. ARVN battalions not lost: memorandum 007's third fewer broken in 1966–67, and this memorandum's further one in eight, within a band that is already at its cap¹¹³⁵². The 1974 appropriation: nothing in this memorandum changes what the Republic asked Congress to pay for or what Congress voted; a better-trained army needs the same ammunition, and the vote stands as history's.

**State of the Republic at the end of 1974.** Phase VI is not revised, but for the record: at the end of 1974 the RVNAF is trained to the man, its posts in the held belt hold better and its wounded live more often, and it is short of ammunition, fuel and pay exactly as before; the course has made the attrition of 1974 somewhat cheaper in lives and has not changed one position the PAVN chose to take.

No phase redraw is proposed. The war's shape has not changed.

## §5 Delta ledger

- **Forces gained:** none in theatre beyond the advisory detachments at the training centres, about 450–750 US spaces in RVN; every soldier of both armies who entered from 1957 is trained.
- **Variables moved by band (chapters):** P1 ARVN.effectiveness +1 → +2 (one band). No other end-vector variable moves in any phase.
- **Variables moved within band (judgments):** US casualties down about 900–1,900 over 1965–72; RVNAF dead down about 4,000–12,000 over 1964–75; ARVN ambush losses down a tenth to a sixth in 1966–67; PF posts with their platoon holding about nineteen attacks in twenty in 1964–65; the 1968 PF sergeant gap a fifth rather than a third; the mobilization's new units a month later; about 600–900 self-defence members killed in the third offensive in three provinces⁵².
- **New events:** EV009-1 (the ten-week cycle and the course number, Phase I), EV009-2 (the mines of Dien Ban, Phase II), EV009-3 (the devaluation and the trained deserters, Phase II), EV009-4 (the self-defence hamlets, Phase III).
- **Altered events:** EV002-2, EV000-18 (Phase I); EV000-23, EV007-3 (Phase II); EV000-42, EV000-51 (Phase III).
- **New categories:** none. The end state is unchanged.

## §6 Changed / not changed

### What has changed

- **The wounded live.** About 5,000–14,000 American and South Vietnamese soldiers who died on the historical course are alive, because every soldier of both armies was taught the tourniquet in basic training; shown at Thuan Loi in June 1965, at Albany in November 1965 and in the first wave of Tet⁵². It is the largest single effect of the provision and no adaptation of the enemy's touches it.
- **The first minute.** The ARVN and the territorial forces are the first government army in this war that the Front cannot reliably break with its opening volley: the ARVN a band steadier in 1964–65, its ambush losses down a tenth to a sixth in 1966–67, and the territorial post holding its first ten minutes nineteen times in twenty.
- **Nobody joins untrained.** The untrained draftee sent straight to his unit is gone from the war, enforced by a number on the muster roll, and the mobilization of 1968 is trained rather than rushed.
- **The sergeants come back.** The drill-sergeant corps the army could not spare in 1964–65 repays it from 1966, and the sergeant gap memorandum 007's field commissions opened in 1968 is a fifth rather than a third.
- **The enemy's war moved.** In the coastal lowlands the mine became the principal killer of American Marines and of farmers; around Saigon the third offensive went for the self-defence groups; and a few hundred trained deserters a year taught the Front's village platoons some of the course.

### What has not changed

- **Who chooses the battle.** The enemy declined the trained unit and attacked the one beside it, as he had declined the rearmed and the well-led ones; the fighting gain appears as fights not fought.
- **The order above the private.** The RF convoy at Bau Bang rode its trucks because its sector commander put it there; the 1st Battalion, 7th Regiment landed where its division told it; the corps commanders of 1972 and 1975 were chosen as before.
- **Desertion and the family.** Men deserted for pay, rice and family, which the course does not touch; the devaluation of 1966 and the roads of March 1975 show it.
- **Unit training and the squad leader.** The battalion's collective training, historically almost nonexistent, is untouched; the binding constraint has moved from the private to the squad leader and the platoon.
- **The clock and the end.** American tolerance, the 1974 appropriation, the critical path of 1975 and the date of the fall are as before. Saigon falls on 30 April 1975, at rung (a).

### Trajectory Log entry

Memorandum 009 trained every private soldier of both armies from 1957, the Americans to their services' 2026 courses, the ARVN to a ten-week course on the same programme and the territorials to six weeks of it, and found that training changes who lives, not who wins. Every soldier carried the tourniquet drill, and about 5,000–14,000 men lived who had died historically, at Thuan Loi, at Albany and in the streets at Tet. The trained post held its first ten minutes nineteen times in twenty; the trained battalion was attacked less, so its gain was invisible. The enemy answered by moving his war to the mine in Quang Nam, to the sapper, and to the self-defence hamlets of 1968, and the devaluation of 1966 sent trained deserters home and some to the Front's guerrillas. The mobilization of 1968 was a month later and trained. Nothing reached the generals, the soldier's family or the aid vote. Saigon falls on 30 April 1975, at rung (a).

### Positions recorded

- **POS009-1** · vars: ARVN.effectiveness, RFPF.effectiveness, ENEMY.engagement_control · Basic training changes how the fights the enemy accepts go and how many wounded live after them, never which fights he accepts; in this war the ARVN of 1964–65 stands a band above history on the course, the carbine and the educated lieutenant together, and the enemy answers the trained soldier with the mine, the sapper and the untrained target (009 §4.P1, §4.P2; EV002-2, EV009-2, EV009-4)
- **POS009-2** · vars: MED.survival, US.casualties, US.political_tolerance · In this war every soldier of both armies is taught the tourniquet in basic training; the saving is a judgment on a rate of two to four percent of killed in action, shown at Albany and in the first wave of Tet, about 900–1,900 Americans and 4,000–12,000 South Vietnamese, below a band on every variable and proof against enemy adaptation (009 §4.P2, §4.P3, §4.N; EV000-23, EV000-42)
- **POS009-3** · vars: ARVN.desertion, ECON.inflation, VCI.strength · Training does not reach desertion, which follows the soldier's pay, rice and family; the trained deserter carries the course home, and a few hundred a year in the Saigon belt carry it to the Front's guerrillas (009 §4.P2; EV009-3)

### Persons recorded

- **Tran Van Hai** · Sergeant Major, senior drill sergeant of the drill-sergeant academy at the Quang Trung Training Center from 1957, a member of its first class · invented in 009 · Trained the drill sergeants of the ten-week course and kept the rule that a recruit takes his orders from a Vietnamese
- **Raymond F. Coyle** · Lieutenant Colonel, US Army; chief of the MACV advisory detachment to the Central Training Command 1964–67 · invented in 009 · Watched and scored the centres and did not teach; backed the refusal of June 1964 to return a third of the drill sergeants to the battalions
- **Pham Van Tho** · Corporal, PF platoon at the Long Huu post, Kien Hoa, trained at the Ben Tre centre in 1962 · invented in 009 · Kept a third of the platoon's ammunition for the second assault on 17 November 1964 and brought his two wounded alive to the Ham Long boats
- **Walter K. Dorsey** · Lieutenant Colonel, USMC; commanding officer, 1st Battalion, 26th Marines, 1965–67 · invented in 006 · Brought the battalion to Dien Ban as a unit; held the breach at La Huan in August 1966; reported in August 1967 that his Marines "do everything right in a firefight and are given none" after a year in which mines killed two-thirds of his dead
- **Vu Ngoc Hoan** · Colonel, commander of the ARVN 25th Division from March 1967 · invented in 007 · The list's second name; moved the dependents' quarters inside the perimeters and paid the ration in rice after the devaluation of 1966, cutting the division's desertion by about a quarter

Source: RVNAF training — GlobalSecurity.org, https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/world/vietnam/rvn-af-training.htm — basic training for Vietnamese soldiers 12 weeks, cut to nine in the 1968 mobilization; 33 training centres in 1968–70 with normal capacity about 65,000 and emergency about 105,655; 26 service schools, 24,000–34,000; 1968 training centre load 168,335 Army and RF recruits, 19,174 OCS preparatory students and 22,483 PF recruits; 588 new and 656 old PF platoons trained in 1968; units filled with hastily drafted men without formal training; training-centre posts given to officers relieved of combat command; 1966 relaxation of advisory standards; leadership the greatest obstacle.
60. Source: Quang Trung National Training Center — Wikipedia, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quang_Trung_National_Training_Center — founded 1953 at Hoc Mon, 16 km north-west of Saigon; mid-1950s capacity about 24,000 a year, 8,000 at a time; eight-week basic course in the mid-1950s; 1959 advisers judged instruction hours, especially marksmanship, insufficient; MACV's January 1964 revision of the basic and advanced individual programmes, completed by March; May 1964 recommendation to extend recruit training to four other national centres and the Ranger centre at Duc My; 1967 relief of the commandant for using trainee labour on an ice plant.
61. Source: Civil Guard (Regional Forces) — GlobalSecurity.org, https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/world/vietnam/rvn-civil-guard.htm — Civil Guard cycle cut from 24 to 12 weeks; 1964 RF recruit course nine weeks matching the ARVN centres; RF refresher course four weeks; about 46,000 Civil Guard and 40,000 Self-Defense Corps in 1959; 1964 RF companies 473 rising to 533, of which 494 trained; 123-man RF companies; 1964 recruiting goal 14,000 with about 2,000 trained; 137,187 RF in 759 companies FY1965–66; pre-1964 units "armed bands of young men".

Source: South Vietnamese Popular Force — Wikipedia, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Vietnamese_Popular_Force — about 50,000 Self-Defense Corps in 1957; 3,892 platoons in June 1965; about 135,000 against 185,000 authorised at end 1965; 4,121 rifle platoons and 242 intelligence squads in October 1967; desertions 11,957 (1962), 18,540 (1963), 36,608 (1964), 49,224 (1965); 27.0 per 1,000 a month in early 1966; training camps cut from 37 to 19 under the Central Training Command; Westmoreland's view that the PF needed little training; RF and PF credited with about 30% of enemy casualties; mostly carbines and BARs in 1967; Westmoreland's 1968 request for 268,000 M16s and 11,200 M79s.
